Alright, I'm faced with writing the equation of a parabola that opens downward. The vertex is given as (5, -2), and the focus is at (5, -4). Okay, so let's start by recalling what I know about parabolas.

First, since the parabola opens downward, it means that the general form we'll use is:

\[
y = a(x - h)^2 + k
\]

Here, \((h, k)\) represents the vertex of the parabola. Plugging in the given vertex (5, -2), we get:

\[
y = a(x - 5)^2 - 2
\]

Now, to find the value of \(a\), I need to use the position of the focus relative to the vertex.

For a parabola that opens downward, the distance between the vertex and the focus is given by \(\frac{1}{4|a|}\). The general formula for this distance is:

\[
p = \frac{1}{4|a|}
\]

In our case, the vertex is at (5, -2) and the focus is at (5, -4). Calculating the distance \(p\) between these two points:

\[
p = |k_{focus} - k_{vertex}| = |-4 - (-2)| = |-2| = 2
\]

Since the parabola opens downward, \(p\) will be negative, so \(p = -2\).

Now, substituting into the equation for \(a\):

\[
-2 = \frac{1}{4|a|}
\]

Solving for \(|a|\):

\[
|a| = \frac{1}{4 \times 2} = \frac{1}{8}
\]

Since the parabola opens downward, we know that \(a\) must be negative. Therefore:

\[
a = -\frac{1}{8}
\]

Finally, substituting \(a\) back into our equation:

\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 5)^2 - 2
\]

To convert from standard form to vertex form, I'll use the method of completing the square. 

1. **Factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) from the first two terms:**
   \[
   y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5
   \]

2. **Find the value to complete the square inside the parentheses:**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( 2/2 = 1 \).

   \[
   y = 3(x^2 + 2x + 1 - 1) + 5
   \]
   This can be rewritten as:
   \[
   y = 3[(x^2 + 2x + 1) - 1] + 5
   \]

4. **Simplify the expression:**
   \[
   y = 3(x + 1)^2 - 3 + 5
   \]
   Combine like terms:
   \[
   y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2

To convert the equation \( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \) into vertex form, follow these steps:

1. **Isolate y**:
   Start by solving for \( y \):
   \[
   8y = -x^2 + 4x - 12
   \]
   Divide both sides by 8:
   \[
   y = -\frac{1}{8}x^2 + \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{3}{2}
   \]

2. **Complete the square**:
   Factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) from the first two terms:
   \[
   y = -\frac{1}{8}(x^2 - 4x) - \frac{3}{2}
   \]
   
   To complete the square inside the parentheses, take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( -4/2 = -2 \), and square it to get 4:
   \[
   y = -\frac{1}{8}\left[(x^2 - 4x + 4) - 4\right] - \frac{3}{2}
   \]
   
   This simplifies to:
   \[
   y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 + \frac{1}{2} - \frac{3}{2}
   \]
   
   Combine the constants:
   \[
   y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1
   \]

The equation is now in vertex form, where the vertex of the parabola is at \( (2, -1) \).

To find the equation of a parabola with its focus at \((0, 3)\) and directrix \(y = 3\), we can follow these steps:

1. **Understand the Definition of a Parabola**:
   A parabola is defined as the set of all points equidistant from a fixed point called the **focus** and a fixed line called the **directrix**.

2. **Identify Given Information**:
   - **Focus**: \((0, 3)\)
   - **Directrix**: \(y = 3\)

3. **Determine the Vertex**:
   The vertex of the parabola lies exactly halfway between the focus and the directrix.
   
   Since both the focus and directrix are on the line \(y = 3\), the vertex is also at \((0, 3)\).

4. **Calculate the Distance from the Vertex to the Focus (p)**:
   The distance \(p\) from the vertex to the focus is calculated as:
   \[
   p = |y_{\text{focus}} - y_{\text{directrix}}| = |3 - 3| = 0
   \]

5. **Determine the Equation of the Parabola**:
   When \(p = 0\), the parabola degenerates to a single point at the vertex, which is also the focus and directrix in this case.
   
   Therefore, every point on the "parabola" is equidistant from \((0, 3)\) and the line \(y = 3\). This results in all points satisfying:
   \[
   y = 3
   \]

6. **Final Equation**:
   The equation of the parabola (which degenerates to a straight line here) is simply:
   \[
   \boxed{y = 3}
   \]

To determine the equation of a circle with endpoints of its diameter at \((-2, 1)\) and \((6, -3)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Center (\(h, k\))
The center of the circle is the midpoint of the diameter. Use the midpoint formula:
\[
h = \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \quad k = \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2}
\]
Plugging in the given points:
\[
h = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = \frac{4}{2} = 2
\]
\[
k = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = \frac{-2}{2} = -1
\]
So, the center is at \((2, -1)\).

### Step 2: Find the Radius (\(r\))
The radius is half the length of the diameter. Use the distance formula to find the distance between the two endpoints and then divide by 2:
\[
\text{Distance} = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]
\[
r = \frac{\sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2}}{2} = \frac{\sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2}}{2} = \frac{\sqrt{64 + 16}}{2} = \frac{\sqrt{80}}{2}
\]
Simplify the radius:
\[
r = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
\]

### Step 3: Write the Equation of the Circle
The standard form of the equation of a circle is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
Substitute \(h = 2\), \(k = -1\), and \(r = 2\sqrt{5}\):
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2
\]
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 4 \times 5 = 20
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the circle is:
\[
\boxed{(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20}
\]

To find the center and radius of the circle given by the equation \( x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0 \), we complete the square for both the \( x \) and \( y \) terms.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0
   \]

2. **Group the \( x \) terms and \( y \) terms together, moving constants to the other side:**
   \[
   (x^2 + 6x) + (y^2 - 4y) = 12
   \]

3. **Complete the square for the \( x \)-terms:**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( 6/2 = 3 \).
   - Square this value: \( 3^2 = 9 \).
   - Add and subtract 9 on the left side:
     \[
     (x^2 + 6x + 9) - 9
     \]
   - This simplifies to:
     \[
     (x + 3)^2 - 9
     \]

4. **Complete the square for the \( y \)-terms:**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( y \), which is \( -4/2 = -2 \).
   - Square this value: \( (-2)^2 = 4 \).
   - Add and subtract 4 on the left side:
     \[
     (y^2 - 4y + 4) - 4
     \]
   - This simplifies to:
     \[
     (y - 2)^2 - 4
     \]

5. **Substitute the completed squares back into the equation:**
   \[
   (x + 3)^2 - 9 + (y - 2)^2 - 4 = 12
   \]

6. **Combine constants on the left side and move them to the right side:**
   \[
   (x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 - 13 = 12
   \]
   \[
   (x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
   \]

7. **Now, the equation is in standard form:**
   \[
   (x - (-3))^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 5^2
   \]
   
8. **Identify the center and radius:**
   - The center of the circle is at \( (h, k) = (-3, 2) \).
   - The radius \( r \) is the square root of 25, which is 5.

**Final Answer:**

The center of the circle is \((-3, 2)\) and the radius is \(5\).

To find the equation of a circle centered at the origin (0,0) and passing through the point (0,5), follow these steps:

1. **Use the standard equation of a circle:**
   The general equation for a circle with center at \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   Since the center is at the origin, substitute \(h = 0\) and \(k = 0\):
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 = r^2
   \]

2. **Substitute the point (0,5) into the equation to find \(r\):**
   Plugging in \(x = 0\) and \(y = 5\):
   \[
   0^2 + 5^2 = r^2 \\
   25 = r^2
   \]
   
3. **Solve for \(r\):**
   Take the square root of both sides:
   \[
   r = \sqrt{25} \\
   r = 5
   \]

4. **Write the final equation with the determined radius:**
   Substitute \(r = 5\) back into the standard equation:
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 = 25
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{x^2 + y^2 = 25}

The equation of the circle passing through the points (3,4), (-2,-5), and (7,8) is found by determining its center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\).

1. **Find the perpendicular bisectors**:
   - For the line segment between (3,4) and (-2,-5):
     - Midpoint: \(\left( \frac{1}{2}, -\frac{1}{2} \right)\)
     - Slope of the segment: 3/5
     - Perpendicular slope: -5/3
     - Equation: \(y + \frac{1}{2} = -\frac{5}{3}\left(x - \frac{1}{2}\right)\)

   - For the line segment between (7,8) and (-2,-5):
     - Midpoint: \(\left( 2.5, 1.5 \right)\)
     - Slope of the segment: \( \frac{13}{9} \)
     - Perpendicular slope: \(-\frac{9}{13}\)
     - Equation: \(y - 1.5 = -\frac{9}{13}(x - 2.5)\)

2. **Solve the system of equations** to find the center:
   - Solving gives \(h = \frac{37}{14}\) and \(k = -\frac{5}{7}\).

3. **Calculate the radius** using one of the points, for example (3,4):
   - \(r^2 = \left(3 - \frac{37}{14}\right)^2 + \left(4 + \frac{5}{7}\right)^2 = \frac{1105}{196}\)

\]

The equation of a circle can be rewritten in standard form by completing the square. 

Given:
x² + y² - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0

Step-by-step solution:

1. Group x terms and y terms:
   (x² - 8x) + (y² - 6y) + 20 = 0

2. Complete the square for each group:
   - For x: Take half of -8, which is -4, square it to get 16.
   - For y: Take half of -6, which is -3, square it to get 9.

3. Add these squares inside the equation and balance by adding them to the right side:
   (x² - 8x + 16) + (y² - 6y + 9) = -20 + 16 + 9

4. Simplify:
   (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 5

Thus, the circle has center at (4, 3) and radius √5.

Answer:  
Final Answer: {'Final Answer': 'Center: (4, 3), Radius: √5'}

To determine the equation of a circle centered at \((1, 2)\) that passes through the point \((4, 6)\), we can follow these steps:

### Step 1: Recall the Standard Equation of a Circle
The standard form of the equation of a circle with center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
In this case, the center \((h, k)\) is \((1, 2)\).

### Step 2: Find the Radius of the Circle
Since the circle passes through the point \((4, 6)\), we can use this point to find the radius \(r\). The distance between the center and this point is equal to the radius.

Using the distance formula:
\[
r = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
r = \sqrt{(4 - 1)^2 + (6 - 2)^2} = \sqrt{3^2 + 4^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5
\]

\[
(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 5^2
\]
Simplifying:
\[
(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
\]

\boxed{\text{None}}

I need to find the eccentricity of the given ellipse. The standard form of an ellipse is \( \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 \), where "a" and "b" are the semi-major and semi-minor axes, respectively.

Next, I'll calculate "a" by taking the square root of 36, which gives me a = 6. Similarly, b² is 9, so b equals 3.

Now, to find the eccentricity (e), I use the formula \( e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{b^2}{a^2}} \). Plugging in the values: \( e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{9}{36}} = \sqrt{\frac{27}{36}} = \sqrt{\frac{3}{4}} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \).

To find the **eccentricity** of the given ellipse:

### Given Equation:
\[
\frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Identify \(a^2\) and \(b^2\):**
   - The standard form of an ellipse is \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\), where \(a > b\).
   - Comparing with the given equation:
     \[
     a^2 = 36, \quad b^2 = 9
     \]

2. **Calculate \(a\) and \(b\):**
   - Take square roots to find \(a\) and \(b\):
     \[
     a = \sqrt{36} = 6, \quad b = \sqrt{9} = 3
     \]

3. **Determine the formula for Eccentricity (\(e\)):**
   The eccentricity of an ellipse is given by:
   \[
   e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{b^2}{a^2}}
   \]
   
4. **Plug in the values:**
   \[
   e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{9}{36}} = \sqrt{1 - \frac{1}{4}} = \sqrt{\frac{3}{4}} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
   \]

5. **Final Result:**
   The eccentricity of the ellipse is:
   \[
   e = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \approx 0.866
   \]

### Final Answer:
\[
\boxed{e = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}}
\]

- Foci: (0, ±5) ⇒ \(c = 5\) and \(c^2 = 25\)

\[

\]
Simplifying:
\[
\frac{4}{b^2} = 1
\]
So,
\[
b^2 = 4
\]
Taking the square root gives \(b = 2\) (again, since b is positive).

Now that I have both \(a\) and \(b\), I can plug these back into the standard form of the ellipse equation.

Therefore, the equation is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{9} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1

I recall that conic sections like hyperbolas have various properties such as center, vertices, foci, and asymptotes. Eccentricity is one of those properties. For hyperbolas, the eccentricity (e) measures how "stretched" or "opened up" the branches are compared to a circle.

I think the formula for eccentricity of a hyperbola involves the distance from the center to a focus divided by the distance from the center to a vertex. So, e = c/a, where c is the distance from the center to a focus and a is the distance from the center to a vertex along the transverse axis.

The hyperbola has its foci along the y-axis at (0, ±5) and vertices at (0, ±3). This indicates a vertical hyperbola. The standard form of such a hyperbola is:

\[
\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Here, \(a\) represents the distance from the center to the vertices, and \(c\) represents the distance from the center to the foci. Using the given points:

- Vertices: (0, ±3) ⇒ \(a = 3\)
- Foci: (0, ±5) ⇒ \(c = 5\)

The relationship between \(a\), \(b\), and \(c\) for hyperbolas is given by:

\[
c^2 = a^2 + b^2
\]

Substituting the known values:

\[
5^2 = 3^2 + b^2 \\
25 = 9 + b^2 \\
b^2 = 16
\]

Now, substituting \(a^2\) and \(b^2\) into the standard form equation yields:

\[
\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1
\]

**Final Answer:**  
The equation of the hyperbola is \(\boxed{\frac{y^2}{9} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1}\).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify Hyperbola Orientation:**
   - Since foci are on the y-axis (at (0, ±√10)), it's a vertical hyperbola.
   
2. **Standard Form:**
   - The equation is \(\frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1\).

3. **Relate C and A:**
   - Distance to foci (c) is √10, so \(c^2 = a^2 + b^2 = 10\).

4. **Plug in the Point (2,3):**
   - Substitute into standard form: \(\frac{9}{a^2} - \frac{4}{b^2}=1\).
   
5. **Set Up System of Equations:**
   - \(u = a^2\) and \(v = b^2\),
     1. \(u + v = 10\)
     2. \(\frac{9}{u} - \frac{4}{v}=1\)

6. **Solve for u and v:**
   - Substitute \(v=10-u\) into the second equation:
     \[
     \frac{9}{u} - \frac{4}{10-u} = 1
     \]
   - Multiply through by \(u(10-u)\):
     \[

     \]
     Simplify to:
     \[

     \]

7. **Final Equation:**
   - \(\frac{y^2}{5} - \frac{x^2}{5}=1\) simplifies to \(y^2 - x^2 =5\).

The equation of the hyperbola is:

Okay, so I need to find the equation of a parabola with its vertex at (0, 0) and focus at (0, 4). Hmm, let's start by recalling what defines a parabola. A parabola is the set of all points that are equidistant from a fixed point called the focus and a fixed line called the directrix. Since the vertex is halfway between the focus and the directrix, I can use this information to find the equation.

First, let's determine the orientation of the parabola. The vertex is at (0, 0), and the focus is at (0, 4). Both points have the same x-coordinate, which means the parabola opens upwards along the y-axis. So, it should be a vertical parabola.

The standard form of a vertically opening parabola with vertex at (h, k) is:
\[ y = \frac{1}{4p}(x - h)^2 + k \]
where p is the distance from the vertex to the focus. Since the vertex is at (0, 0), this simplifies to:
\[ y = \frac{1}{4p}x^2 \]

Now, I need to find the value of p. The focus is given as (0, 4). The general formula for the focus in a vertical parabola is (h, k + p). In our case, h is 0 and k is 0, so the focus should be at (0, 0 + p) which means p = 4.

Wait, that makes sense because the distance from the vertex to the focus is 4 units upwards. So substituting p into the equation:
\[ y = \frac{1}{4*4}x^2 \]
which simplifies to:
\[ y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \]

Let me double-check this. If I plug in x=0, then y should be 0, which it does since the vertex is at (0, 0). Also, if I take a point on the parabola, say when x=4, solving for y gives:
\[ y = \frac{1}{16}*(4)^2 = \frac{16}{16}=1 \]
So the point (4, 1) should be on the parabola. Let me verify if it's equidistant from the focus (0,4) and the directrix.

The distance from (4,1) to the focus (0,4) is:
√[(4-0)^2 + (1-4)^2] = √[16 + 9] = √25 = 5

Now, what's the equation of the directrix? For a vertical parabola opening upwards with vertex at (0,0) and p=4, the directrix should be y = -p, so y = -4. The distance from (4,1) to the directrix is |y - (-4)| = |1 + 4| = 5.

Yes, both distances are equal, which confirms that my equation is correct.

So, putting it all together, the equation of the parabola is:
\[ y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \]

***********************<think>
Alright, let's tackle this problem step by step. The goal is to find the equation of a parabola with a vertex at (3, 4) and a directrix at y = 2.

**Step 1: Understanding Parabolas**

A parabola is defined as the set of all points that are equidistant from a fixed point called the focus and a fixed line called the directrix. The vertex of the parabola lies exactly midway between the focus and the directrix.

**Step 2: Identifying Given Information**

From the problem:
- Vertex (h, k) = (3, 4)
- Directrix y = 2

Since the directrix is a horizontal line, this tells us that the parabola opens upward or downward. The vertex lies between the focus and the directrix.

**Step 3: Determining the Direction of Opening**

Given:
- Vertex at (3, 4)
- Directrix at y = 2

The directrix is below the vertex, so for a parabola that opens upward, the focus would be above the vertex. For it to open downward, the focus would be below the vertex.

But since we don't have information about the focus, let's proceed with the standard form of the equation based on direction.

**Step 4: Choosing the Appropriate Standard Form**

The standard form of a parabola opening upward or downward is:
\[ (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k) \]

Where:
- (h, k) is the vertex
- p is the distance from the vertex to the focus (and also from the vertex to the directrix)

**Step 5: Determining the Value of 'p'**

The distance between the vertex and the directrix will give us |p|. Since the directrix is below the vertex, p will be positive if the parabola opens upward.

Given:
- Vertex y-coordinate = 4
- Directrix y = 2

Distance from vertex to directrix (|p|):
\[ |p| = \text{distance} = 4 - 2 = 2 \]

Since the directrix is below the vertex, p should be positive. So,
\[ p = 2 \]

**Step 6: Plugging Values into the Standard Form**

Now, plug h = 3, k = 4, and p = 2 into the standard form equation:
\[ (x - 3)^2 = 4(2)(y - 4) \]
Simplify:
\[ (x - 3)^2 = 8(y - 4) \]

**Step 7: Final Equation of the Parabola**

So, the equation in standard form is:
\[ (x - 3)^2 = 8(y - 4) \]

If we want to express it in slope-intercept form for clarity, we can expand and simplify:

\[ y = \frac{1}{8}(x - 3)^2 + 4 \]

**Step 8: Verifying the Equation**

Let's verify that the vertex is indeed at (3, 4):
- The equation is already in the standard form with h = 3 and k = 4.

First, recall that a parabola is defined as the set of all points that are equidistant from the focus and the directrix. This means for any point \( (x, y) \) on the parabola, the distance to the focus must equal its perpendicular distance to the directrix.

**Step 1: Distance from Point to Focus**
The distance between a point \( (x, y) \) and the focus \( (2, 3) \) can be calculated using the distance formula:

\[
\text{Distance} = \sqrt{(x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2}
\]

**Step 2: Distance from Point to Directrix**
The directrix is a vertical line at \( x = -1 \). The perpendicular distance from any point \( (x, y) \) to this line is simply the horizontal distance:

\[
\text{Distance} = |x - (-1)| = |x + 1|
\]

**Step 3: Set Distances Equal and Square Both Sides**
To eliminate the square root, we'll set the two distances equal and then square both sides of the equation:

\[
\sqrt{(x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2} = |x + 1|
\]

Squaring both sides gives:

\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = (x + 1)^2
\]

**Step 4: Expand and Simplify the Equation**
Expand each term:

\[
(x^2 - 4x + 4) + (y^2 - 6y + 9) = x^2 + 2x + 1
\]

Combine like terms:

\[

\]

Subtract \( x^2 \) from both sides and simplify further:

\[
-4x + 4 + y^2 - 6y + 9 = 2x + 1
\]

Bring all terms to one side to set the equation to zero:

\[
-4x - 2x + 4 + 9 - 1 + y^2 - 6y = 0
\]

Combine like terms:

\[
y^2 - 6y - 6x + 12 = 0
\]

**Step 5: Rearrange the Equation**
To express the equation in a standard form, we can rearrange it as follows:

\[
y^2 - 6y = 6x - 12
\]

Finally, to make it more presentable, divide both sides by 6 if needed. However, \( y^2 - 6y = 6x - 12 \) is a valid form of the equation.

**Final Answer:**
\boxed{y^{2} - 6y = 6x - 12}

The vertex form of a parabola provides insights into its vertex coordinates. For the given equation \( y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \), we can convert it to vertex form by completing the square.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[
   y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5
   \]

2. **Factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) from the first two terms:**
   \[
   y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5
   \]

3. **Complete the square inside the parentheses:**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( 1 \).
   - Square it to get \( 1^2 = 1 \).

4. **Add and subtract this square inside the parentheses:**
   \[
   y = 3[(x^2 + 2x + 1) - 1] + 5
   \]
   This simplifies to:
   \[
   y = 3(x + 1)^2 - 3 + 5
   \]

5. **Combine the constants outside:**
   \[
   y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
   \]

6. **Identify the vertex form and vertex coordinates:**
   The equation is now in the form \( y = a(x - h)^2 + k \), where the vertex \( (h, k) \) is at \( (-1, 2) \).

**Final Answer:**  
The vertex form of the parabola is \( y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2 \).

To convert the quadratic equation \( x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \) into vertex form, we complete the square for the \( x \)-terms.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[
   x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0
   \]

2. **Rearrange the terms to group \( x \)-terms together and isolate \( y \):**
   \[
   x^2 - 4x = -8y - 12
   \]

3. **Complete the square for the quadratic in \( x \):**

   - The coefficient of \( x \) is \(-4\). Half of this coefficient is \(-2\).
   - Square \(-2\) to get \( 4 \).

   Add and subtract this square inside the equation:
   \[
   (x^2 - 4x + 4) - 4 = -8y - 12
   \]

4. **Rewrite as a perfect square trinomial:**
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 - 4 = -8y - 12
   \]

5. **Move constants to the right side:**
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 = -8y - 12 + 4
   \]
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 = -8y - 8
   \]

6. **Factor out \(-8\) from the right side:**
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1)
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The vertex form of the equation is:

The problem asks for the equation of a parabola with focus at (0, 3) and directrix y = 3. 

To find this, we set up an equation where the distance from any point (x, y) to the focus equals its distance to the directrix.

1. Distance from (x, y) to focus (0, 3):
   \[
   \sqrt{(x - 0)^2 + (y - 3)^2}
   \]
2. Distance from (x, y) to directrix y = 3:
   \[
   |y - 3|
   \]

Setting these equal:

\[
\sqrt{x^2 + (y - 3)^2} = |y - 3|
\]

Squaring both sides:

\[
x^2 + (y - 3)^2 = (y - 3)^2
\]

Simplifying:

\[
x^2 = 0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad x = 0
\]

Thus, the equation of the parabola is \( x = 0 \), which is a vertical line. This represents a degenerate parabola.

Answer: \( x = 0 \)

To find the equation of the parabola that passes through the points \((2, 8)\), \((-1, 3)\), and \((0, 4)\), we'll follow these steps:

### Step 1: Use the Point at \(x = 0\)
Given the point \((0, 4)\):
\[
y = ax^2 + bx + c
\]
Substituting \(x = 0\) and \(y = 4\):
\[
4 = a(0)^2 + b(0) + c \\
\boxed{c = 4}
\]

### Step 2: Formulate Equations Using Other Points

**Point (2, 8):**
Substitute \(x = 2\) and \(y = 8\) into the equation:
\[
8 = a(2)^2 + b(2) + 4 \\
8 = 4a + 2b + 4
\]
Simplify:
\[
4 = 4a + 2b \quad \text{(Equation 1)}
\]

**Point (-1, 3):**
Substitute \(x = -1\) and \(y = 3\) into the equation:
\[
3 = a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + 4 \\
3 = a - b + 4
\]
Simplify:
\[

\]

### Step 3: Solve the System of Equations

From **Equation 2**:
\[
a = b - 1 \quad \text{(Expression for \(a\))}
\]

Substitute \(a = b - 1\) into **Equation 1**:
\[
4 = 4(b - 1) + 2b \\
4 = 4b - 4 + 2b \\
6b = 8 \\
b = \frac{4}{3}
\]

Now, find \(a\):
\[

a = \frac{4}{3} - 1 \\
a = \frac{1}{3}

The equation of the circle with endpoints of its diameter at (-2, 1) and (6, -3) is found by first determining the center (midpoint) and then calculating the radius.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Find the Center (Midpoint):**
   The midpoint formula is:
   \[
   h = \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \quad k = \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2}
   \]
   Plugging in (-2, 1) and (6, -3):
   \[
   h = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = 2, \quad k = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = -1
   \]
   So, the center is at (2, -1).

2. **Calculate the Radius:**
   The distance between the two endpoints gives the diameter. Using the distance formula:
   \[
   \text{Distance} = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
   \]
   Plugging in the points:
   \[
   \text{Diameter} = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80} = 4\sqrt{5}
   \]
   The radius is half the diameter:
   \[
   r = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
   \]

3. **Write the Equation of the Circle:**
   Using the standard form \((x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2\) with center (2, -1) and radius \(2\sqrt{5}\):
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2 = 4 \times 5 = 20
   \]

To convert the general form of the circle equation \( x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 \) into its standard form, we'll follow these steps:

### Step 1: Group Like Terms
Start by grouping the \( x \)-terms and \( y \)-terms together:
\[
(x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
\]

### Step 2: Complete the Square for \( x \)-Terms
The coefficient of \( x \) is \(-4\). To complete the square:

1. Take half of \(-4\), which is \(-2\).
2. Square it to get \( (-2)^2 = 4 \).

Add and subtract this value inside the equation:
\[
x^2 - 4x + 4 - 4
\]
This simplifies to:
\[
(x - 2)^2 - 4
\]

### Step 3: Complete the Square for \( y \)-Terms
The coefficient of \( y \) is \(2\). To complete the square:

1. Take half of \(2\), which is \(1\).
2. Square it to get \( (1)^2 = 1 \).

Add and subtract this value inside the equation:
\[
y^2 + 2y + 1 - 1
\]
This simplifies to:
\[
(y + 1)^2 - 1
\]

### Step 4: Combine and Simplify
Substitute the completed squares back into the original equation:
\[
(x - 2)^2 - 4 + (y + 1)^2 - 1 = 11
\]
Combine like terms:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 - 5 = 11
\]
Add \(5\) to both sides:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
\]

### Final Answer
The standard form of the circle's equation is:

To find the equation of the circle with the given information:

**Given:**
- **Center (h, k):** (-2, 5)
- **Point on the circle:** (1, 9)

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Find the Radius (r):**
   
   The radius is the distance from the center to any point on the circle. We can use the distance formula:
   
   \[
   r = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
   \]
   
   Plugging in the given points (-2, 5) and (1, 9):
   
   \[
   r = \sqrt{(1 - (-2))^2 + (9 - 5)^2} = \sqrt{(3)^2 + (4)^2} = \sqrt{9 + 16} = \sqrt{25} = 5
   \]

2. **Write the Equation of the Circle:**
   
   The standard form of the equation of a circle with center (h, k) and radius r is:
   
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   
   Substituting the known values:
   
   \[
   (x - (-2))^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 5^2 \\
   (x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25
   \]

The solution is \(\boxed{h = -\dfrac{113}{31}}\) and \(\boxed{k = \dfrac{63}{62}}\).

To solve the system of equations:

\[ 7h + k = 5 \]
\[ h + 9k = 4 \]

we start by multiplying the first equation by 7 to facilitate elimination:

\[ 49h + 7k = 35 \]

Next, we multiply the second equation by 3:

\[ 3h + 27k = 12 \]

We then add these two resulting equations together to eliminate \( h \):

\[ 49h + 7k + 3h + 27k = 35 + 12 \]
\[ 52h + 34k = 47 \]

To simplify, we divide the entire equation by 2:

\[ 26h + 17k = 23.5 \]

Next, we use the elimination method again with different multipliers. We multiply the first original equation by 9 and the second by 1 to eliminate \( k \):

\[ 63h + 9k = 45 \]
\[ h + 9k = 4 \]

Subtracting these equations:

\[ (63h + 9k) - (h + 9k) = 45 - 4 \]
\[ 62h = 41 \]
\[ h = \frac{41}{62} = -\frac{113}{31} \]

Substituting \( h = -\frac{113}{31} \) back into the first original equation:

\[ 7\left(-\frac{113}{31}\right) + k = 5 \]
\[ -\frac{791}{31} + k = 5 \]
\[ k = 5 + \frac{791}{31} \]
\[ k = \frac{155 + 791}{31} \]
\[ k = \frac{946}{31} = \frac{63}{62} \]

Thus, the solution is:

\[
\boxed{h = -\dfrac{113}{31}} \quad \text{and} \quad \boxed{k = \dfrac{63}{62}}
\]

The given equation represents a circle. To find its center and radius, we rearrange it into the standard form by completing the square.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[ x^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0 \]

2. **Group \(x\) and \(y\) terms:**
   \[ (x^2 - 8x) + (y^2 - 6y) + 20 = 0 \]
   
3. **Move the constant term to the other side:**
   \[ x^2 - 8x + y^2 - 6y = -20 \]

4. **Complete the square for \(x\) terms:**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \(x\): \(-8/2 = -4\)
   - Square it: \((-4)^2 = 16\)
   - Add and subtract 16:
     \[ x^2 - 8x + 16 = (x - 4)^2 \]

5. **Complete the square for \(y\) terms:**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \(y\): \(-6/2 = -3\)
   - Square it: \((-3)^2 = 9\)
   - Add and subtract 9:
     \[ y^2 - 6y + 9 = (y - 3)^2 \]

6. **Add the squares to both sides:**
   \[ (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = -20 + 16 + 9 \]
   
7. **Simplify the right side:**
   \[ (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 5 \]

8. **Identify center and radius from standard form \((x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2\):**
   - Center (\(h, k\)) is \((4, 3)\)
   - Radius \(r\) is \(\sqrt{5}\)

**Final Answer:**

```

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the General Equation:**
   The standard form of a circle's equation is \((x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2\), where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

2. **Substitute the Center Coordinates:**
   Given center at (1, 2), substitute into the equation:
   \[
   (x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = r^2
   \]

3. **Find the Radius \(r\):**
   The circle passes through the point (4, 6). Substitute x=4 and y=6 into the equation to find \(r\):
   \[
   (4 - 1)^2 + (6 - 2)^2 = r^2 \\
   3^2 + 4^2 = r^2 \\
   9 + 16 = r^2 \\
   r^2 = 25
   \]

   \[
   (x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
   \]

For an ellipse, the relationship between \( a \), \( b \) (the semi-minor axis), and \( c \) is given by the equation \( c^2 = a^2 - b^2 \). Plugging in the known values:

\]

\[
900 = 2500 - b^2 \\
b^2 = 2500 - 900 \\
b^2 = 1600 \\

b = 40
\]

\frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{1600} = 1

### Step 1: Understand the Standard Form of an Ellipse
The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin (0, 0) with a horizontal major axis is:
\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
where:
- \( a \) is the semi-major axis length,
- \( b \) is the semi-minor axis length.

\[

\]

### Step 3: Identify the Distance from the Center to the Foci (\( c \))
The foci are located at (±30, 0), so the distance \( c \) is:
\[
c = 30
\]

### Step 4: Find the Semi-Minor Axis Length (\( b \))
For an ellipse, the relationship between \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \) is given by:

c^2 = a^2 - b^2
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the orientation of the ellipse:**
   - Vertices are at (0, 7) and (0, -7), which lie on the y-axis.

   - Midpoint = ((0 + 0)/2, (7 + (-7))/2) = (0, 0).

3. **Find 'a' (semi-major axis):**
   - Distance from center to a vertex along the major axis is 'a'.
   - From (0,0) to (0,7), so a = 7.

4. **Find 'c' (distance from center to focus):**
   - Distance from center to either focus is 'c'.
   - From (0,0) to (0,5), so c = 5.

5. **Calculate 'b' using the ellipse relationship:**
   - For an ellipse, a² = b² + c².
   - Plug in known values:
     - 7² = b² + 5²
     - 49 = b² + 25
     - Solve for b²: b² = 49 - 25 = 24.

6. **Write the standard equation of the ellipse:**
   - For a vertical ellipse centered at (0,0), the standard form is:
     x²/b² + y²/a² = 1
   - Plugging in the values:
     x²/24 + y²/49 = 1.

**Answer:**  
The equation of the ellipse is:

Step-by-step explanation:

To write the equation of an ellipse with a center at (4, -3), a vertical major axis of length 14, and a horizontal minor axis of length 10, follow these steps:

1. **Identify the standard form of the ellipse**:
   Since the major axis is vertical, the standard form of the equation is:
   \[
   \frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} = 1
   \]
   where (h, k) is the center, \( a > b \), and the major axis length is \( 2a \).

2. **Determine the values of \( h \), \( k \), \( a \), and \( b \)**:
   - The center is given as (4, -3), so \( h = 4 \) and \( k = -3 \).
   - Major axis length is 14 ⇒ semi-major axis \( a = 14 / 2 = 7 \).
   - Minor axis length is 10 ⇒ semi-minor axis \( b = 10 / 2 = 5 \).

3. **Plug the values into the standard form**:
   Substitute \( h = 4 \), \( k = -3 \), \( a^2 = 49 \), and \( b^2 = 25 \) into the equation.

   The resulting equation is:
   \[
   \frac{(x - 4)^2}{25} + \frac{(y + 3)^2}{49} = 1
   \]

This step-by-step explanation shows how to derive the equation of an ellipse given its center, major axis length, and minor axis length.

Okay, so I need to find the asymptotes and foci of the hyperbola given by the equation x²/9 - y²/16 = 1. Hmm, let's start by recalling what I know about hyperbolas.

First, the standard form of a hyperbola depends on whether it opens horizontally or vertically. The general form is either (x-h)²/a² - (y-k)²/b² = 1 for horizontal opening or (y-k)²/b² - (x-h)²/a² = 1 for vertical opening. In this case, our equation is x²/9 - y²/16 = 1, which matches the first form, so it's a horizontally opening hyperbola centered at (h,k). From the equation, h and k are both zero because there are no shifts in the terms, so the center is indeed at (0,0).

Now, for the asymptotes of a hyperbola. I remember that the equations for the asymptotes of a horizontal hyperbola are y = ±(b/a)x. Here, 'a' and 'b' are the denominators under x² and y² respectively when the equation is in standard form.

Looking at our equation: x²/9 - y²/16 = 1. So, a² is 9, which means a is √9 = 3. Similarly, b² is 16, so b is √16 = 4. Therefore, the slopes of the asymptotes are ±(b/a) = ±(4/3). Thus, the equations of the asymptotes should be y = (4/3)x and y = -(4/3)x.

Next, let's find the foci. For hyperbolas, the distance from the center to each focus is given by c, where c² = a² + b². We already have a² as 9 and b² as 16, so c² should be 9 + 16 = 25. Taking the square root of 25 gives c = 5.

Since this is a horizontally opening hyperbola, the foci are located along the x-axis at (±c, k). But since our center is at (0,0), k is zero. Therefore, the foci are at (5,0) and (-5,0).

1. **Identify the direction**: The directrix is below the vertex, so the parabola opens upwards.

   \[
   (x - 3)^2 = 8(y - 4)
   \]

   \[
   y = \frac{1}{8}(x - 3)^2 + 4
   \]

The equation of the parabola is \( (x - 3)^2 = 8(y - 4) \) in standard form, or equivalently, \( y = \frac{1}{8}(x - 3)^2 + 4 \) in vertex form.

   - Vertex: \( (h, k) = (0, 3) \)
   - Focus: \( \left(h + a, k\right) = \left(\frac{7}{4}, 3\right) \), so \( a = \frac{7}{4} \)

   - The focus is to the right of the vertex, indicating the parabola opens towards the positive x-direction.

3. Use the standard form of a horizontally opening parabola:
   - The equation is \( (y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h) \)
   - Here, \( p \) is the distance from the vertex to the focus: \( p = \frac{7}{4} \)

   - Simplify: \( (y - 3)^2 = 7x \)

The given quadratic equation in standard form can be converted to vertex form by completing the square. Here's a step-by-step solution:

### Given Equation:
\[ x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \]

### Step 1: Isolate \( y \)
Subtract 12 from both sides:
\[ x^2 - 4x + 8y = -12 \]
Subtract \( x^2 - 4x \) from both sides:
\[ 8y = -x^2 + 4x - 12 \]

### Step 2: Solve for \( y \)
Divide all terms by 8:
\[ y = \frac{-x^2}{8} + \frac{4x}{8} - \frac{12}{8} \]
Simplify the fractions:
\[ y = -\frac{1}{8}x^2 + \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{3}{2} \]

### Step 3: Complete the Square
Factor out \( -\frac{1}{8} \) from the first two terms:
\[ y = -\frac{1}{8}(x^2 - 4x) - \frac{3}{2} \]
Complete the square inside the parentheses by adding and subtracting \( (-4/2)^2 = 4 \):
\[ y = -\frac{1}{8}\left[(x^2 - 4x + 4) - 4\right] - \frac{3}{2} \]
\[ y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 + \frac{1}{8}(4) - \frac{3}{2} \]
Simplify:
\[ y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 + \frac{1}{2} - \frac{3}{2} \]
Combine constants:
\[ y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1 \]

### Final Answer in Vertex Form
The vertex form of the equation is:
\[ y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1 \]
The vertex (h, k) is:
\[ \boxed{\left\{ 'Final Answer' : 'y = -\\dfrac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1'} \right\} } \]

To determine the equation of a parabola that passes through the points (2, 8), (-1, 3), and (0, 4), we'll use the general form of a quadratic equation:

\[ y = ax^2 + bx + c \]

**Step 1: Substitute Each Point into the Equation**

1. **For the point (0, 4):**
   \[
   4 = a(0)^2 + b(0) + c \\
   \Rightarrow c = 4
   \]
   
2. **For the point (2, 8):**
   \[
   8 = a(2)^2 + b(2) + 4 \\
   8 = 4a + 2b + 4 \\
   \Rightarrow 4a + 2b = 4 \quad \text{(Equation 1)}
   \]
   
3. **For the point (-1, 3):**
   \[
   3 = a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + 4 \\
   3 = a - b + 4 \\
   \Rightarrow a - b = -1 \quad \text{(Equation 2)}
   \]

**Step 2: Solve the System of Equations**

We have:
\[
\begin{cases}
c = 4 \\
4a + 2b = 4 \quad \text{(Equation 1)} \\
a - b = -1 \quad \text{(Equation 2)}
\end{cases}
\]

**Solving for \( a \) and \( b \):**

From **Equation 2**:
\[
a = b - 1
\]

Substitute \( a = b - 1 \) into **Equation 1**:
\[
4(b - 1) + 2b = 4 \\
4b - 4 + 2b = 4 \\
6b - 4 = 4 \\
6b = 8 \\
b = \frac{4}{3}
\]

Now, substitute \( b = \frac{4}{3} \) back into \( a = b - 1 \):
\[
a = \frac{4}{3} - 1 = \frac{1}{3}
\]

**Step 3: Write the Final Equation**

Substituting \( a = \frac{1}{3} \), \( b = \frac{4}{3} \), and \( c = 4 \) into the general form:
\[
y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4
\]

**Final Answer:**

{"Final Answer": "y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4"}
```

For the y terms \( y^2 + 2y \), I take half of 2, which is 1, and square it to get 1.

To convert the general form of a circle's equation \( x^2 + y^2 - 4x + 2y - 11 = 0 \) into its standard form, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Group Like Terms
First, group the \( x \) terms and the \( y \) terms together:
\[
(x^2 - 4x) + (y^2 + 2y) = 11
\]

### Step 2: Complete the Square for \( x \)
To complete the square for the \( x \) terms, take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( -4 \):
\[
\left( \frac{-4}{2} \right)^2 = (-2)^2 = 4
\]
Add and subtract this value inside the equation:
\[
(x^2 - 4x + 4) - 4
\]

### Step 3: Complete the Square for \( y \)
Similarly, complete the square for the \( y \) terms. Take half of the coefficient of \( y \), which is \( 2 \):
\[
\left( \frac{2}{2} \right)^2 = (1)^2 = 1
\]
Add and subtract this value inside the equation:
\[
(y^2 + 2y + 1) - 1
\]

### Step 4: Rewrite and Simplify
Now, rewrite the original equation with the completed squares:
\[
(x^2 - 4x + 4) + (y^2 + 2y + 1) = 11 + 4 + 1
\]
Simplify both sides:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16
\]

### Final Answer
The standard form of the circle's equation is:
\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer}: \{(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 16\}}
\]

To determine the equation of a circle centered at the origin that passes through the point (0, 5), I start by recalling the standard form of a circle's equation.

The general equation for a circle with its center at (h, k) is:
\[ (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2 \]
Since the center is at the origin, both \( h \) and \( k \) are 0. This simplifies the equation to:
\[ x^2 + y^2 = r^2 \]

Next, I need to find the radius of the circle. The radius can be found by calculating the distance from the center (origin) to any point on the circumference. In this case, the point given is (0, 5).

I calculate the distance using the distance formula:
\[ \text{Distance} = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2} \]
Plugging in the coordinates of the origin (0, 0) and the point (0, 5):
\[ r = \sqrt{(0 - 0)^2 + (5 - 0)^2} = \sqrt{0 + 25} = \sqrt{25} = 5 \]

Now that I have the radius, I can substitute it back into the simplified equation:
\[ x^2 + y^2 = 5^2 \]
\[ x^2 + y^2 = 25 \]

Therefore, the equation of the circle is \( x^2 + y^2 = 25 \).
</think>

To find the equation of a circle centered at the origin (0, 0) that passes through the point (0, 5), follow these steps:

1. **Recall the Standard Equation of a Circle:**

   The general form of the equation for a circle with center at (h, k) is:
   
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   
   Where \( r \) is the radius of the circle.

2. **Apply the Center Coordinates:**

   Since the center is at the origin (0, 0), substitute h and k with 0:
   
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 = r^2
   \]
   
3. **Determine the Radius \( r \):**

   The circle passes through the point (0, 5). Use this point to find \( r \):
   
   Substitute x = 0 and y = 5 into the equation:
   
   \[
   0^2 + 5^2 = r^2 \\
   25 = r^2
   \]
   
   Therefore, \( r^2 = 25 \).

4. **Write the Complete Equation:**

   Substitute \( r^2 \) back into the equation:
   
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 = 25
   \]
   
**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{x^2 + y^2 = 25}

To determine the points of intersection between the line \( y = x + 3 \) and the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \), I'll follow these steps:

First, substitute \( y \) from the line equation into the circle equation:
\[
x^2 + (x + 3)^2 = 17
\]
Expanding the squared term gives:
\[
x^2 + x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17
\]
Combine like terms:
\[
2x^2 + 6x + 9 - 17 = 0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad 2x^2 + 6x - 8 = 0
\]
Divide the entire equation by 2 to simplify:
\[
x^2 + 3x - 4 = 0
\]
Next, solve this quadratic equation using the quadratic formula:
\[
x = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a}
\]
Here, \( a = 1 \), \( b = 3 \), and \( c = -4 \):
\[
x = \frac{-3 \pm \sqrt{9 + 16}}{2} = \frac{-3 \pm \sqrt{25}}{2} = \frac{-3 \pm 5}{2}
\]
This yields two solutions for \( x \):
\[
x_1 = \frac{-3 + 5}{2} = 1, \quad x_2 = \frac{-3 - 5}{2} = -4

To determine the points of intersection between the line \( y = x + 3 \) and the circle \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Substitute \( y \) from the Line Equation into the Circle Equation
Start by substituting \( y = x + 3 \) into \( x^2 + y^2 = 17 \):

\[
x^2 + (x + 3)^2 = 17
\]

### Step 2: Expand and Simplify the Equation
Expand the squared term:

\[
x^2 + x^2 + 6x + 9 = 17
\]
Combine like terms:

\[
2x^2 + 6x + 9 - 17 = 0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad 2x^2 + 6x - 8 = 0
\]

Divide the entire equation by 2 to simplify:

\[
x^2 + 3x - 4 = 0
\]

### Step 3: Solve the Quadratic Equation
Use the quadratic formula \( x = \frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^2 - 4ac}}{2a} \), where \( a = 1 \), \( b = 3 \), and \( c = -4 \):

\[
x = \frac{-3 \pm \sqrt{9 + 16}}{2} = \frac{-3 \pm 5}{2}
\]

This yields two solutions:

\[
x_1 = \frac{-3 + 5}{2} = 1, \quad x_2 = \frac{-3 - 5}{2} = -4
\]

### Step 4: Find Corresponding \( y \)-Values
Substitute these \( x \)-values back into the line equation \( y = x + 3 \):

- For \( x = 1 \):
  
  \[
  y = 1 + 3 = 4
  \]
  
  So, one point is \( (1, 4) \).

- For \( x = -4 \):
  
  \[
  y = -4 + 3 = -1
  \]
  
  So, the other point is \( (-4, -1) \).

### Final Answer

The points of intersection are:

\[
\boxed{\left( 1, 4 \right)} \quad \text{and} \quad \boxed{\left( -4, -1 \right)}
\]

Given the major axis length is 10, the semi-major axis \(a\) is half of that, which is 5. So, \(a^2 = 25\).

Similarly, the minor axis length is 6, so the semi-minor axis \(b\) is 3, and thus \(b^2 = 9\).

Substituting these values into the standard equation gives \(\frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\).

To determine the equation of an ellipse centered at (0, 0) with a horizontal major axis and specified lengths for both axes, follow these steps:

### **Step 1: Understand the Parameters**

- **Center:** The center is at the point \((h, k)\), which in this case is \((0, 0)\).
  
- **Major Axis:** Horizontal, meaning it extends along the x-axis. Its length is \(2a\).

- **Minor Axis:** Vertical, extending along the y-axis. Its length is \(2b\).

### **Step 2: Identify Given Values**

From the problem statement:

- Length of the major axis (\(2a\)) = 10
- Length of the minor axis (\(2b\)) = 6

### **Step 3: Calculate Semi-Axis Lengths**

Divide each axis length by 2 to find \(a\) and \(b\):

\[
a = \frac{2a}{2} = \frac{10}{2} = 5
\]
\[
b = \frac{2b}{2} = \frac{6}{2} = 3
\]

### **Step 4: Plug Values into the Standard Ellipse Equation**

The standard form of an ellipse with a horizontal major axis is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Substituting \(a\) and \(b\):

\[
\frac{x^2}{5^2} + \frac{y^2}{3^2} = 1
\]
\[
\frac{x^2}{25} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

### **Final Answer**

The equation of the ellipse is:

Alright, so I'm faced with finding the eccentricity of an ellipse given by the equation \( \frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \). Hmm, okay. I've studied ellipses before, but let me try to recall the key details.

First, I remember that the standard form of an ellipse is \( \frac{(x-h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y-k)^2}{b^2} = 1 \), where (h,k) is the center, and 'a' and 'b' are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes, respectively. I need to figure out which one is larger because that determines whether it's a horizontal or vertical ellipse.

Looking at the given equation: \( \frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \). So here, under x² we have 36, and under y² we have 9. That means \( a^2 = 36 \) and \( b^2 = 9 \). To find 'a' and 'b', I take the square roots.

Calculating:
- \( a = \sqrt{36} = 6 \)
- \( b = \sqrt{9} = 3 \)

Since 6 > 3, this is a horizontal ellipse. Now, eccentricity (e) measures how elongated the ellipse is and is given by the formula:

\[
e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{b^2}{a^2}}
\]

Plugging in the values:
- \( b^2 = 9 \)
- \( a^2 = 36 \)

So,
\[
e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{9}{36}} = \sqrt{1 - \frac{1}{4}} = \sqrt{\frac{3}{4}} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
\]

Therefore, the eccentricity of the ellipse is \( \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \).
</think>

- The distance from the center to each focus (\(c\)) is \(5\) units. Thus, \(c = 5\).

\[

follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the Standard Form of an Ellipse
The standard form of an ellipse's equation is:

\[
\frac{(x-h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y-k)^2}{b^2} = 1,
\]

where:
- \((h, k)\) is the center of the ellipse.
- \(a\) and \(b\) are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes.

### Step 2: Compare with the Given Equation
Given:

\[

\]

we can see that:
- \(a^2 = 4\) ⇒ \(a = 2\)
- \(b^2 = 9\) ⇒ \(b = 3\)

Substituting the values of \(a\) and \(b\):
